The hub then sends `background` with the session, the entry's pid and `busy` (the roster lists the process `busy`, which counts as `running` while hooks are off and the state waits for the 30-second read), and answers 200 with the moving process's `pid` once the flow stored the move, else 409 with the refusal. `POST /background/check {hostPid, pid}` (`Runtime.windowAlone`) answers whether that process is still the only live Claude process under `hostPid`, and refuses (409) unless a session's move waits for that pid with at least 10 seconds of its 30-second wait left, counted from the move's start, so the window's close leaves the process time to exit before the wait gives up. `resume-session` polls the pid with `process.kill(pid, 0)` every 250 ms until 30 seconds after the move began, then sends `move-cancelled`; it launches nothing once the session's `move` is another attempt's or gone. It sends `move-launching` with a deadline 60 seconds on, then runs `claude --bg --resume <native ID> <prompt>` in the session's `cwd` with no other flag, so Claude wakes the session with the options it started with and keeps its IDs. Output naming `started a copy as <id>` stops that copy and fails the move. Otherwise the hub reads `claude agents --json` every 2 seconds until an entry with the session's ID, `kind: background` and a pid gives `session-resumed` with its short ID; a failed read or an entry without a pid decides nothing, and the deadline gives `resume-failed` with the launch's error or "The session did not appear in claude agents within 60 seconds." A result for a move that Delete ended stops the process it started and is logged. After a restart, `startup` runs a `waiting` move again from the pid wait and only confirms a `launching` one by its stored deadline, never launching twice.
- Stop: `claude stop <id>`, 15-second timeout. A failed stop leaves the session stoppable. Never `claude rm`, which can delete the worktree. Only a `release` stop checks the roster first; an operator stop and an `open` stop do not.
- Open in terminal (PRD R28): `controlsOf` in `packages/hub/src/rows.ts` adds a `terminal` window command `{sessionId, cwd, name, shortId, active}` for the latest session: with its short ID while it runs in its background process, else `shortId: null`, with `active` true for a session that is released but not ended, which the roster lists as interactive, as in a terminal or a VS Code tab. A starting session gets none, and neither does an `external` one (PRD R81). With `dock.openVSCode: always` and `dock.openTerminal: vscode` the dock leaves it for the item window through `POST /window`; with `review` or `app` it runs in the dock's terminal, and with `auto` in either ([Dock](#dock)). `openTerminal` in `extensions/switchboard/src/open.ts` shows the terminal the window opened for that mode and session while it runs, including after the extension host restarted: `TerminalKeys` in `src/terminalKeys.ts` keeps each key's terminal process ID in workspace state (`switchboard.terminalPids`) and takes the listed terminal that runs it, because a reconnected terminal has no `shellPath` or `shellArgs` to match (M89); otherwise it opens an `active` session with Open in VS Code, because resuming a live session starts a copy (M18), and opens a VS Code terminal named `Switchboard · <ticket key>` running `claude attach <short ID>`, or `claude --resume <session ID>` in `cwd`, for the others. Leaving an attach keeps the session running (M7, M9), but release still stops an attached session when its turn ends. The roster lists a resumed session as interactive, so it is never released.
- Open in VS Code (from a History link, PRD R59, and in an item window, PRD R29): resumes the session or focuses it where `claudeCode.preferredLocation` puts it. With `sidebar`, the window calls `claude-vscode.editor.open(<native session ID>, …, { programmatic: 'honor-preferred-location' })`, which opens it in Claude's sidebar unless a tab already holds it (GC M63); otherwise `claude-vscode.primaryEditor.open(<native session ID>)`, which always uses a tab. Claude's tab and sidebar list only the sessions of its window's first folder, and opening another session there starts an empty one (GC M6, M7, M52). So the window posts `{sessionId, cwd, window}` to the hub's `POST /open`, which answers `{workspace, here}`, the destination window's workspace file or, for a folder-hosted conversation, its folder, and whether that is the asking window's binding: when it is, the window claims the open at once and opens the session, else it opens that file or folder with `vscode.openFolder` and `forceNewWindow`, which focuses the window already on it; a session of the window's own folder goes through the hub too, since another work item can share the folder, as Support's do. A terminal command the hub already handed this window opens a session of its folder directly. Every pending open names its destination window (architecture.md § Window scope): the asking window, when the session belongs to one of its conversations; else the workspace file the dock names; else the session's own window, its host's for a hosted conversation, read from the store when the snapshot no longer lists the work item; one with none is refused with 409 "The session has no window to open it in." A workspace a caller names must be the session's own window, or the open is refused with 409 "The session opens only in its own window."; only a session the store does not know goes where the caller names. A window claims what was left for it with `POST /claim {folder, workspace, focused, window}` when it connects to the hub, whenever it gains focus, and on a `claim` event, and opens it; the hub hands an open only to the window whose key's binding is the destination, never by a folder or a workspace the claim names, so a folder window claims only its own folder's, an item window never a folder window's, and two item windows on one folder, as for Support, never take each other's; `focused` is VS Code's window focus. The reply also holds `commands`, the dock's pending window commands (`POST /window`) for the workspace file the claim's window key binds, never one the claim only names, and while `focused` also those for any window, oldest first, so a window reconnecting in the background does not take what the dock left for the window it brought forward. The window runs `openTabs` before opening any claimed session and the others (`openChanges`, `openTerminal`, History, `openLink`) after it; a failure there or in a command shows its error and the rest still run. `/window` accepts only `changes`, `terminal`, `history`, `log` and `openUrl` commands with the fields their handlers read, and `openTabs` with a workspace file, and a pending command expires after 2 minutes. An open the dock posts names its item window's workspace file, and only a claim with that file takes it (PRD R71). A pending open expires after 2 minutes. `WindowQueue` in `packages/hub/src/windowQueue.ts` holds the pending opens and commands and writes them, with the next command ID, to `<Switchboard home>/window-queue.json` on each change, and a starting hub reads that file, because the first window started after an extension update replaces the hub before it claims what the dock left for it. `/open` and `/window` answer 500 when the file cannot be written, so no command ID is handed out twice; a claim or withdrawal is kept in memory even then. A pending open is kept per destination window, the newest replacing an earlier one. The hub logs each session left and claimed.

### Code Mode sessions

Experimental, started only with `sessions.codeMode` on (PRD R93–R95). `CodeModeRunner` in `packages/claude/src/codeMode.ts` implements `SessionRunner` for Claude sessions with `Session.codeMode` set, with the [terminal holder](#terminal-holder)'s client and the marker reader `ClaudeRunner` uses. The runtime holds four runners and selects app-server for `session.execution: "app-server"`, otherwise the Claude holder for `session.codeMode`, otherwise the legacy provider runner wherever it now indexes its runners by provider (start, stop, reconcile, observe, markers, `retiredCopy`, `contract`, `register`). Experiments: [mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M63–M83.

- **Setting.** `Flow.startSession` reads `sessions.codeMode` ([Settings](#settings)) for each new Claude session: setup, task and approved-prompt sessions, ad-hoc, task-report and scheduled runs. For a Code Mode start it stores `codeMode`, the Claude session ID and the launch ID, two UUIDs from the flow context's `newUuid`, on the `starting` session and passes them in `start-session`, so a hub restarted during the start finds the process in the holder (`reconcile`); `session-start-failed` clears both, so a failed start is never resumed and Retry starts a new session. Codex sessions never start in Code Mode. A resume keeps the session's own `codeMode`. With `sessions.installHooks` off the start fails with "Code Mode sessions need sessions.installHooks."
- **Command.** `HolderLink.find(true)`, which starts the holder when none runs, then the holder's `spawn` of `claude --session-id <uuid> --permission-mode <mode> -n <name> [--model <model>] --append-system-prompt <contract, panel, link and conversation sections> --settings <json> <prompt>` in `cwd`, the prompt left out when empty ([Hosted conversations](#hosted-conversations)), with no shell and no `--bg`, so no `bgIsolation`. After the contract, `--append-system-prompt` carries `PANEL_PROMPT`: the "Code References in Text" and "Focus view in this editor" sections of the system prompt the Claude VS Code extension appends to its panel, copied word for word from its `extension.js` (version in the constant's comment), so a session writes as the panel does; the panel's "User Selection Context" and "Who you are writing for" sections are left out. `codeModePrompt(artifactsDir)` then adds Switchboard's own "Links in this terminal" section, which tells the session to start each relative file link target with `./`, as in `[filename.ts:42](./src/filename.ts#L42)`, because VS Code's terminal makes a relative path clickable only so (mechanics M83), and `conversationRules` (`packages/claude/src/conversationRules.ts`), the conversation rules with the session's artifact folder ([Conversation](#conversation)). `--settings` also sets `tui: "default"`, the classic renderer, whose finished output scrolls into the scrollback the screen copy reads, over a user setting of `fullscreen`, and `viewMode: "focus"`, which starts the terminal in Focus view and adds the CLI's "Focus mode" prompt section, the condition of the panel's Focus view section, as the panel's `claudeCode.focusView` does. The holder holds it under the Switchboard session ID, which a Claude session ID change does not touch, with the label `<work item key> <Claude session ID>`, the IDs known at the spawn. The hub makes the UUID, so `nativeSessionId` and `shortId` are known before the start (mechanics M63).
- **Environment.** `codeModeEnvironment` builds the process environment from the hub's: without `ELECTRON_*` (including `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`), `VSCODE_*`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, Claude's session markers (the dock's `Terminals.SessionMarkers` and claude-collab's `cleanEnv` list; other `CLAUDE_CODE_*` settings pass through), `CLAUDE_CODE_NO_FLICKER`, which would force the full-screen renderer over `tui`, and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` and `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS`; with `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_CODE_MODE=1`, `SWITCHBOARD_LAUNCH=<launch ID>` and `CLAUDE_ARTIFACTS_DIR=<session folder>/.wip/artifacts/w<work item ID>` (`artifactsDir` in core), which the start and the resume make, with a `.gitignore` of `*` so no artifact is committed, before the spawn ([Artifacts](#artifacts)); with an absolute `sessions.claudePath`'s folder first on PATH; and `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` as inherited. User settings `env` outranks the process environment, so the gateway is skipped by `--settings` `{"env": {"ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL": "https://api.anthropic.com", "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY": "", "ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN": "", "ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS": ""}, "viewMode": "focus", "tui": "default"}` (mechanics M64). An `apiKeyHelper` in user settings cannot be overridden, so Claude starts without Code Mode and the start check fails it. Nested Claude runs inherit the environment but not `--settings`: a nested `claude -p` runs without Code Mode through the gateway, with the parent's `SWITCHBOARD_LAUNCH`, and a nested `claude --bg` gets neither variable (mechanics M77).
- **Start check.** Waits up to 60 seconds after the spawn. It succeeds when the holder's `screen` head, with cursor moves read as spaces, other escape sequences removed and its lines joined across the terminal's wraps, holds `Code Mode (experimental) is on` (mechanics M63) and a marker of this session ID and launch exists; any event counts, because `SessionStart` comes first and the marker is replaced on every event. The start fails, and the process is killed, on the text `Code Mode (experimental) isn't available:`, with or without its leading `⚠` and across the terminal's line wrap (mechanics M64, M71), with Claude's line as the cause, or on a marker of this session ID and launch while the retained output lacks `Code Mode (experimental) is on`. A process exit fails it with the screen's last non-empty lines. Whichever of the two lines comes first counts, since later output can quote either. A holder read that fails is tried again until the 60 seconds pass, when the process is killed. A live process with a dialog on screen (`Enter to confirm`), or with no marker of its launch after 20 seconds, is waiting on a dialog (mechanics M70): the start succeeds with `startPrompt`, the status line says to choose "Yes" if Claude asks about Code Mode, and the evidence is checked again when its first marker arrives. A process that exits at the prompt ends the session.
- **Liveness and state.** One holder `list` per roster read: a session is alive while the holder lists its process live, and ended once a successful `list` does not. A failed `list` fails only this runner's read; the runtime applies the other runners' observations. The roster read skips entries whose pid the holder holds, so a held session is never classed `interactive` (mechanics M67). Markers count only with the session's ID and launch, so a killed launch's last marker is never read (mechanics M68). The background mapping applies, with `UserPromptSubmit` of `source: "poll_event"` meaning `running`, `SessionStart` `startup` `running`, since the prompt runs as the first turn, or `idle` for a session started with no prompt (`Session.promptless`, mechanics M87), `SessionStart` `resume`, `clear` or `fork` `idle`, since a promptless resume and an ID change wait for input, and `SessionStart` `compact` leaving the state as it was, because `/compact` typed in the terminal ends with no `Stop` while an automatic compaction runs mid-turn (mechanics M65, M68, M73). `AskUserQuestion` exists in Code Mode, so the contract keeps it (mechanics M65). While `sessions.installHooks` is off, a held session keeps its last state and detail, and its row says it is unobserved, derived from the setting and not stored; turning hooks back on resumes observation from its markers.
- **End rule** (PRD R95). A held session keeps running between turns: no turn end releases it, whatever its kind or its work item's state, so a setup session whose task has started, a session of a done work item and an older session of its work item stay held (`Flow.endHeld` releases only an app-server thread's subscription). The hub, not the holder, ends a held session by a release (`stop-session` with reason `release`, which leaves the work item's status as it is) only through the idle end, the flow event `held-idle`, which needs a roster read to find the holder's `clients` at 0 and the session's `startedAt`, `lastInputAt` and `lastOutputAt` older than `CODE_MODE_IDLE_MS`, 1 hour, and a turn end, except for a session that is unobserved because `sessions.installHooks` is off, which needs none; a session waiting on its usage limit is never ended so ([Rate limits](#rate-limits)). While a terminal is attached that end is deferred: the runtime asks the holder for the session's `clients` before it ends it, runs one end per session at a time, and at each roster read tries again every held session left `releasing`, including one a stopped hub left. Before it ends the session the runtime reads its marker again: one that is not a turn end, such as a `PreToolUse` that replaced the prompt's marker, skips the end, except a compaction (`SessionStart` `compact`), which says neither, because a manual `/compact` ends with no `Stop`: after one, any input or output within `INPUT_SETTLE_MS` of now defers the end, since a running turn keeps writing to its terminal and the turn end's own time is gone; and input after the time the turn-end marker records defers it for `INPUT_SETTLE_MS`, 60 seconds, since it may be a prompt whose marker has not been written yet. A new turn before then, such as one typed in the attached terminal, cancels it; an end that fails leaves the session held, and a late failure of an end that already succeeded changes nothing. A deferred end never holds back the next session's start, because R17 and R38 count only sessions running a turn. `Flow.release` for a Code Mode session sets `releasing` only; `released` is set when the holder confirms the exit, so no observation takes a pending end for an ended session. An idle held session costs about 430 to 480 MB working set and 690 to 760 MB private memory with the operator's MCP servers (mechanics M82).
- **Attach serialization.** Ending and attaching are serialized per session in the hub (`Runtime.attach`). `POST /attach` refuses an unknown session (404) and a session that is not Code Mode with "Session <short ID> is not a Code Mode session; open it in Claude.", then takes an attach reservation for 15 seconds (`ATTACH_RESERVATION_MS`), which a refusal drops. A release reads the session's terminals and then defers while a reservation is unexpired, as while a client is attached, logging "a terminal is attaching"; a roster read after the reservation expires tries the end again, when the attached client keeps deferring it. Every end or stop of a held session, an operator Stop included, is tracked until its result has reached the flow, and the attach waits for each before it decides; one that starts during the attach's own waits (the holder lookup, the roster read, the process check) makes it wait and decide again from the holder lookup. The attach then looks up the holder with `HolderLink.find(true)` and answers at once while the holder lists the session live, `starting` included, so a prompt before the start can be answered; otherwise, unless the store says `ended`, it waits for one roster read, which ends a session the holder no longer lists, and refuses one still not ended with "Session <short ID> is still ending; try again." (or "still starting"). Concurrent attaches of one session share one attempt, so it is resumed once. A roster read leaves out a session whose resume spawn runs, and drops its results for a session that was being resumed, got a new launch or ended, as by Stop, while the read ran, so neither a listing taken before the resume nor one that sees the new process under the old launch changes the session; the next read and its markers decide it. The answer's `port` and `token` are those of the holder the hub is connected to (`HolderLink.record`).
- **Stop.** The holder's `kill`, which closes the session's pseudo-terminal, so Claude ends its shell commands, ends the process tree if `claude` has not exited 5 seconds later ([Ending a session](#ending-a-session)), and answers once it exits, within 15 seconds; then the work item is `blocked` "Stopped by you." as for a background session (PRD R40). Offered while the holder lists the process live, after the turn too. `Flow.delete` stops a Code Mode session whenever the holder lists its process live, whatever `released` says: a `starting` one, which the hub kills in the holder by its Switchboard session ID, and ends again once its start completes, since the spawn may come after the stop, and one the operator resumed through Open in terminal, which is held. The row offers Delete while such a session starts too, and its confirmation starts "Its running session is stopped." A failed stop leaves the process listed as an orphan.
- **Resume.** `POST /attach` for an ended session refuses with "Code Mode sessions need sessions.installHooks." while hooks are off, applies the resume guard, then runs `CodeModeRunner.resumeHeld`: a `spawn` of `claude --resume <id> --permission-mode <mode> [--model <model>] --append-system-prompt <panel, link and conversation sections> --settings <json>` with the start's environment, a new launch ID and no prompt, which reads as idle (mechanics M68), labelled as a start, `<session name> <Claude session ID>`. Claude resends the start's recorded system prompt, contract, panel and link sections included, until a compaction, after which the next process renders it from its own flags (mechanics M30); so the resume appends the panel, link and conversation sections, the contract hook restores the contract, and the contract hook's `resume` run gives the session the current conversation rules at once. The mode and model are `launchOptions`'s for the session's kind and its work item's playbook with the current settings, as a start of that work item would use. Before the spawn the hub stores the new launch on the still-ended session with the flow event `held-resuming`, and passes it to the spawn, so a hub that stops during the spawn finds the process under it: an attach, or a roster read for a session already ended when the read began, that finds an ended Code Mode session live in the holder sends `held-resumed` with the stored launch and logs it. A failed spawn refuses the attach with its error and leaves the unused launch stored, which the next resume replaces. When the session is gone once the spawn returns, as after Delete, or is no longer `ended`, the hub ends the new process in the holder and refuses the attach: 404 "No session <id>.", else "Session <short ID> changed while it resumed; try again." The flow event `held-resumed` makes the session `idle` with the new launch and clears `endedAt`, `released`, `releasing` and `lastTree`; its outcome and its work item's status stand until a new turn, which `setState` treats as a resumed session. History says "Session <id> resumed in the terminal holder". A resume Claude refuses shows Claude's message in the terminal, which replays the exited session's screen; planned: on the status line too. Never while the holder lists the session live, because resuming a live session starts a copy (mechanics M18).
- **Resume guard.** A Bash command can outlive its `claude` outside its process tree (mechanics M69). The roster read stores the last non-empty `tree` the holder's `list` gave per session in `Session.lastTree`, pids with creation times. Before a resume, `CodeModeRunner.leftovers` reads the process table once, and any member still running with the same creation time, `claude` or not, refuses it with "Session <short ID> still has <n> processes from its previous run (pids …). Stop them or wait for them to end."; a failed read refuses it with the read's error. Stop of a work item whose latest session is an ended Code Mode session with a stored tree sends `stop-session` with `leftovers`, and the runner ends the leftovers one per round: each round reads the process table and ends, with `killTree`, one member still running with the same creation time, which ends the members under it, because a kill can take long enough for another member to exit and its pid to be reused. It picks a member with no running member above it, where a parent counts only when it was created before its child, since Windows keeps a dead parent's pid as its children's parent and can reuse it; failing that, the earliest created. A Stop reads the table once more than it kills, and runs at most one round per member and one more; success clears `lastTree` and changes nothing else, and a failure shows "Stop failed: Process <pid> could not be ended: …" on the work item. While the guard's last refusal of the latest session stands, the hub keeps its pids in memory and the snapshot's work item carries them as `leftovers`, so the row offers Stop on the ended session; a resume or a successful leftover Stop clears them, and a hub restart forgets them until the next refused attach. A process started after the last read or that left the tree before it is missed; a container that would end it is deferred (PRD Q10).
- **Holder loss.** A holder whose lock is free is gone, and the hub sends the flow event `holder-lost` for each active session it held, not the roster's end path, so `endRun`'s `finished` rule never applies to interrupted work: a session running a turn or waiting on the operator ends with "The terminal holder stopped." and its work item is `blocked` with it, setup included, or returns to `awaiting-approval` with it when its approval was held, as after a Stop; a session whose turn had ended, or that reported done, is `released` and its work item's status and any `approvalHeld` stand, so a done report waiting for its second read still settles; a `starting` session fails its start with the cause. Nothing resumes it until the operator opens it. A holder whose record does not answer while its lock is held is a failed read. Every holder lookup that finds the lock free ends the active Code Mode sessions this way, except those still `starting`, whose own start check settles them, so a start that replaces a gone holder before the next roster read does not hide the loss; a start waiting for its own holder, or replacing one of another protocol, reports none.
- **Identity** (PRD R78, R93). Code Mode sessions are left out of `forkTargets`, `forkOf` and `relation`, so no fork is followed. A report whose native ID is not the session's is refused with "Session <id> is not the session Switchboard started, so the report was not recorded." (`retiredCopy`), and `contract` answers only for the session's own native ID. `/clear`, `/branch` and `/resume <id>` in the terminal give `SessionEnd` of the old ID and `SessionStart` with `source` `clear`, `fork` or `resume` and a new ID, from the same pid and launch (mechanics M73); `/fork` is refused. A marker with the session's launch, another ID and `startSource` `clear`, `fork` or `resume`, once the session's own marker is gone (its `SessionEnd` removes it) and the roster lists the held process, or a member of its tree, with that ID (mechanics M67), moves the session to the new ID as a fork does; a nested resumed run inherits the launch, so the roster decides: the old ID goes to `retiredIds`, and `nativeSessionId` and `shortId` take the new one; a retired ID the held process resumes, as `/resume` after `/clear` does, is followed back and leaves `retiredIds`. A marker with the launch, another ID and no such `startSource` is a nested `claude -p` (mechanics M77) and changes nothing. `/register` refuses a native ID whose marker carries the launch of an active Code Mode session, with "This session runs in the terminal holder for <key>, which Switchboard follows there."
- **Reconcile.** `CodeModeRunner.reconcile` lists the holder and applies the start check to each `starting` session, from the retained head of the output, so a hub restart during a start reads what the start printed; a session the holder does not hold fails as now. Held processes whose `session`, the Switchboard session ID, is no active session of the store are orphans ([Visibility](#visibility-of-held-sessions)); none is killed.
- **Open and terminal.** A Code Mode session's terminal command, for every state including `starting` and `ended`, is `{provider: 'claude', sessionId, cwd, name, shortId, active: false, codeMode: true}` (`codeModeTerminal` in `rows.ts`), whose `sessionId` is the Switchboard session ID and `name` the ticket key: the window or the dock runs `<sessions.nodePath> "<home>/hub.js" attach <Switchboard session ID>` (the hub sets `executable` and `args` for it), titled as for a background session. The dock's terminal name ends with the Switchboard session ID in place of the short ID, so the name and the terminal's reuse survive `/clear`, `/branch` and `/resume`. Its main control is Open in terminal, or Answer in terminal with `review`, wherever a background session's is Open in Claude: `waitsInTerminal` counts any wait of a Code Mode session, so `openQuestion` offers no Open in Claude, and the flow refuses `open-question`. The main control's tooltip and the start and permission prompt tooltips name the Code Mode session; the row shows no mark for it (PRD R22). With `dock.openVSCode: always`, a click on a session that is `starting` or `running` only switches to the item window; any other click on a held or ended session (`openOf`) is a `session` open with the Switchboard session ID, which opens its attach terminal in the item window. With `review`, rule 2 covers every held session, a releasing one included, and rule 6 attaches with resume. `/claim` turns a claimed `session` open (`POST /open` from a click, History's link or a claim) that names a Code Mode session by its Switchboard or Claude session ID into that `terminal` window command, ahead of the other commands, because the Claude view refuses Code Mode, and logs "session <id> claimed by <window> as its attach terminal".
- **Exclusions.** `Flow.background` refuses a Code Mode session with "A Code Mode session runs in the terminal holder, which keeps it running when the window closes." The debrief scheduler skips Code Mode sessions and logs it once per session, because `claude -p --resume` refuses them (mechanics M74). Triage, the report command and the contract hook are unchanged (mechanics M65, M66).

## Codex runner

New sessions use `AppServerRunner` in `packages/codex`; `Session.execution: "app-server"` selects it in the runtime. Null or absent keeps the existing exec runner or Claude holder behavior. The app-server migration adds the nullable execution column and an index on native session IDs. The provider and execution method travel with stop actions, including Delete after the row is gone.

- **Daemon and connection.** One `AppServer` client per hub is shared by the runner, conversation adapter and debrief scheduler. It runs `codex app-server daemon start` under the session's Codex home, then connects through `codex app-server proxy`. The proxy forwards raw bytes: the client uses WebSocket framing over its stdio duplex stream. The daemon owns process lifetime; closing the proxy or the hub never stops it. Switchboard never runs daemon stop, restart or update. Transport errors reject outstanding calls without replaying them. Connection generations invalidate stale requests and subscriptions. Codex's home, sign-in and configuration are inherited; tests and live acceptance inject a separate home.
- **Threads.** A start creates a thread with per-thread permissions, model, report contract, conversation rules, artifact environment and persona tone, records its native ID before submitting the prompt, and sends the prompt with a client message ID. A promptless hosted start leaves it idle. Native thread and turn events decide state; hooks do not. Reads rejoin after connection loss and confirm starts from history. An unconfirmed start stays starting with a visible cause and recovery controls; its prompt is never submitted again automatically. A missing individual thread does not prevent other sessions being observed.
- **Lifetime.** Idle task threads stay available until `Runtime.wake` finds one whose turn ended `CODE_MODE_IDLE_MS` (1 hour) ago and sends `held-idle`, which releases its subscription as a Code Mode session's idle end does, never while the thread waits on its usage limit ([Rate limits](#rate-limits)); a message resumes it. Setup, done and superseded sessions release their subscription after a turn; release does not interrupt newly started work. Stop interrupts the named active turn and unsubscribes only that thread, leaving other sessions and the daemon running. A stopped current thread observed active again takes a new launch identity.
- **Resume.** `Runtime.resumeThread` resumes an ended session that is its work item's latest: `AppServerRunner.configure` sends `thread/resume` with the start's folder, permissions, artifact environment and model from `launchOptions`, which a thread the daemon loads again from disk takes and a loaded thread keeps its own sandbox against (mechanics M95), then injects the current instructions when their hash differs from the stored one. The flow event `held-resumed` then makes the session `idle` under a new launch, clearing `endedAt`, `released` and `releasing` and keeping its outcome and its work item's status until a turn. It waits for a Stop of the session still running, leaves the thread again when a newer session started meanwhile, and leaves the thread unjoined, so the conversation's next follow rejoins it and the daemon replays a request that arrived while the session read as ended. Concurrent resumes share one attempt, and roster reads skip a session while it resumes. `ConversationService.send` calls it for a message to an ended app-server session, whose `SessionView.conversation` stays `full`, and sends the message under the new launch, or gives it back `not-sent` with why. A Codex SessionStart hook with `source: "resume"` for an ended app-server session, as from Open in terminal's `codex resume --remote`, reaches `/contract`, which calls `Runtime.threadResumedElsewhere` and resumes it the same way. Subscription revisions invalidate history readers after an unsubscribe.
- **Conversation.** `CodexConversationAdapter` imports native history and completed item events into `ConversationLog`; native item IDs occupy the existing `transcriptUuid` identity fields and replies use the durable `transcript` source, with no invented transcript lines. It buffers live items during replay, confirms typed messages by `clientId`, and leaves tool results out. Shared scope, history, events, delivery, unread, artifacts and speech remain in `ConversationService`. `ClaudeConversationAdapter` owns transcript lookup, screen polling, guarded input and recovery. Adapters poll independently, so a slow Codex request cannot delay Claude reads.
- **Input and recovery.** `turn/start` accepts input at idle or during a turn. Requests carry the stored message ID as `clientUserMessageId`; history returns `clientId`. A known refusal or unsent write returns the draft; a transport failure never decides a thread's state. A lost response is reconciled from history; unknown delivery stays visible as `uncertain`, with at most three automatic checks per connection or completed turn. A message a stopped hub had not attempted returns to its draft. Input is never replayed automatically. Selected skills carry native skill inputs; image mentions carry local image inputs. Compact is an API operation. Current instructions are injected as a developer context item when a resume needs an update and after a completed `contextCompaction` item, because resume overrides are ignored for loaded threads and compaction can drop injected updates (mechanics M92). The session stores their delivered hash and compaction ID; history detects compaction missed while disconnected, and unchanged instructions are not injected again on each restart. Per-thread serialization prevents overlapping resume and compact operations from injecting the same update twice.
- **Requests.** Native requests carry connection-scoped IDs and thread identity. Only the current window can answer through `/v1/conversation/answer`; the adapter validates pending requests and offered choices. Questions and command/file approvals appear as buttons in the sidebar; explicit VS Code choices return answers. Unsupported request types use the native terminal. Rejoining replays pending requests (mechanics M92).
- **Open and terminal.** A claimed app-server open focuses Switchboard's Conversation view. Open in terminal runs `codex resume --remote unix://<Codex home>/app-server-control/app-server-control.sock <thread>` against the same daemon. The item window uses the hub-supplied arguments; it never hands a live app-server thread to the independent Codex extension. Native terminals and the sidebar can coexist.

### Legacy exec sessions

`CodexRunner` in `packages/codex` implements `SessionRunner` for Codex (mechanics M31). The runtime builds one runner per provider and sends each action to the runner its `provider` names: `start-session` and `stop-session` carry it, because a deleted session's row is gone when its stop runs. The roster read and the marker pass ask each runner about its own sessions; when one runner's read fails, its sessions stay as they were, and the other runner's read still applies. Startup installs both runners' hooks, and a hook error shows each runner's message. Every Codex process gets `CODEX_HOME` set to Codex's home, `CODEX_HOME` as the hub inherited it or `~/.codex`, so the runs and the hook trust read the same hooks.json. Each Codex process the hub starts (runs, hook trust, debriefs) runs `sessions.codexPath`, read at each spawn: `codex` by default, looked up on the hub's PATH. The hub inherits the PATH of the VS Code window that started it, which can lack a Codex installed through nvm, so such an install needs its full path. An absolute path's directory goes first on those processes' PATH, because npm's launcher runs `node` from PATH and nvm keeps `node` beside it. A run, the hook trust and a debrief that cannot find the CLI report `Codex was not found at <path>. Install it or set sessions.codexPath.`; a missing working directory, which also fails with ENOENT, is not reported so. Open in terminal runs `sessions.codexPath` the same way.

- **Runs.** A session's background run is one `codex exec` process the hub spawns detached, with stdin closed, no shell, and stdout and stderr in `<home>/codex/<sessionId>.jsonl|.err`. The run record `<home>/codex/<sessionId>.json` (`RUN_RECORD_VERSION` 2) holds the work directory, mode, model and contract, the thread ID, the spawn time and the process; a record of another version is not read: its session ends at the next roster read without its process being ended, and a stop or open of it fails with that reason. A process is named by its PID and creation time together, because PIDs are reused: on Windows one `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process` query per roster read gives the creation time as a file time; on Linux `/proc/<pid>/stat` gives the start time in clock ticks since boot, with no command run. The run's process is Codex itself (`isCodex`: `codex.exe` or `codex`), the process the hook script finds. On Linux `codex` on PATH is npm's Node launcher, which starts Codex as a child with the same arguments, so a start whose spawned process is not Codex records the tagged Codex process instead, or leaves it to be found by its tag (mechanics M38). Ending a process ends its descendants at once: `taskkill /T /F` on Windows, `SIGKILL` on Linux to the process and each descendant found in `/proc`, the root only while its start time matches the identity the runner checked, and each member only while its start time still matches the scan's, so a PID taken by another process meanwhile is spared. A start that times out looks for the Codex process by its tag when it has no identity yet, so a launcher's child that started late is ended too. Every command line carries `-c switchboard_run="<sessionId>"`, which Codex ignores, so a hub that stopped between a spawn and writing its PID finds the process by its tag.
- **Arguments.** `codex exec --json <mode> -c developer_instructions=<contract> -c switchboard_run=<tag> [-m <model>] -C <cwd> -- <prompt>`. Each mode is `-c` overrides (`modeArgs`): `auto` is the workspace-write sandbox with network and escalations reviewed automatically, `plan` read-only, `dontAsk` workspace-write with network and no escalation, `bypassPermissions` no sandbox; `default` and `acceptEdits` need a person and are refused. Commands in the sandbox cannot use the operator's credentials, so skills that call `gh` need `bypassPermissions`. A prompt that starts with `/<skill>` starts with `$<skill>`, which Codex injects (`codexPrompt`).
- **Start.** Writes the record, spawns, writes the process, then waits up to 60 seconds for `thread.started`: the full thread ID is both the native and the short ID, and History shows its last 8 characters. A process whose creation time cannot be read at once is waited on by its PID and found later by its tag. A process that exits first fails the start with the last line of its stderr, such as Codex's refusal outside a git repository; one that prints no thread in time is ended. After a hub restart, `reconcile` finds an untracked process by its tag and completes the start when its log holds the thread, waits for a live process, and fails it otherwise; when the process query fails it answers `pending`, and the runtime asks again at each roster read.
- **State.** `reduce` in `src/reducer.ts` is the only place that decides a Codex session's liveness and state; the roster read (`observe`, with the process query) and the marker pass (`markers`, with `process.kill(pid, 0)` and the creation times the last query read) both call it. In order: a hook marker written since the run was spawned by a live process other than the run's means the operator resumed the thread, as in the VS Code panel, and the session is interactive, with the marker's state, ended by the rollout's `task_complete` or `turn_aborted` for its turn because Codex fires no hook for a failed turn; a question whose reporting turn has ended keeps the session `waiting`; a live run is running until its log holds the turn's end; a turn end is listed until the session is released, so it is delivered once even when the process exited first; otherwise the session has ended. A marker reading that only the roster read can decide, such as a process that disappeared or a new interactive owner, carries `recheck` and no state.
- **Stop.** A release or an open waits up to 15 seconds for the run's process to exit, since its turn has ended, then ends it; an operator stop ends it at once. Either ends a process only after reading its creation time again, so a reused PID is never ended. An interactive session is never stopped.
- **Serialization.** Every read and change of a session's record runs in that session's queue, after a refresh that finds the run's process by its tag when the record holds none. A failed process query is returned as the error and changes nothing.
- **Open and terminal.** A row's `session` and `question` opens and History links carry the provider, and `/claim` answers the provider of the session it hands over, from the store. The item window checks and activates `openai.chatgpt`, resolves `vscode://openai.chatgpt/local/<thread>` (using `vscode.env.uriScheme`) through `vscode.env.asExternalUri` to target that window, then calls `vscode.open` on the result (mechanics M43). Codex reveals its sidebar and selects the thread; any existing editor tab stays open. Missing or disabled extensions and thrown activation, URI-resolution or open errors are shown by the caller. VS Code owns the URI-handler confirmation; Switchboard neither grants it nor treats a completed request as an acknowledgement that history loaded. Open in terminal runs `codex resume <thread>` in the session's directory once the background run has ended, and is not offered while it runs, because Codex has no attach. Controls, the dock's menu and History say Open in Codex for a Codex session; the dock runs the open on a question row by its `open-question` action, not its label.
- **Questions** (PRD R70). A Codex session's contract offers the `question` report in place of `AskUserQuestion`. The flow stores it on the session with a new ID while the session runs in its background process, and ignores it from a session the operator resumed; the reducer makes the session `waiting` once the reporting turn has ended. The row offers Open in Codex only while the session waits on a reported question, not on a permission prompt. A roster read across which a question was reported is not applied to that session, and the next read decides it. An open stop, an interactive owner and the session's end clear the question; an open stop leaves the work item's status as it was, as for Claude.
- **Hooks.** With `sessions.installHooks`, `installHooks` writes `<home>/codex-hook.mjs` and adds synchronous entries to `<CODEX_HOME or ~/.codex>/hooks.json` for SessionStart, UserPromptSubmit, PreToolUse, PermissionRequest, PostToolUse, Stop and SessionEnd, and `<node> "<home>/contract.mjs"` on SessionStart, which acts only on `source: "compact"` and `source: "resume"` because Codex hooks have no matcher in use; each command runs `<node> "<script>"`, where `<node>` is `sessions.nodePath`; it keeps other entries, finds its own as a Node.js command whose only argument is its script, and updates only that entry in a group it shares, removes the entries of another Switchboard home whose hub does not run, as in Claude's settings, and refuses a file that is not a JSON object; an asynchronous Stop hook does not run before an `exec` process exits. It then runs the trust exchange with `codex app-server` (`trustHooks`), which trusts only entries whose command is Switchboard's. A change to `sessions.codexPath` or `sessions.nodePath` runs this install and trust again (a `sessions.nodePath` change also reinstalls Claude's hooks), so its hook error clears without a hub restart. Installs of one runner run in turn, so an older result never replaces a newer one. Codex runs these hooks for every Codex session on the machine, so the hook first looks for a run record in `<home>/codex/` that names the thread, and exits for any other thread. For a thread of Switchboard's, the hook writes `<home>/activity/<thread>.json` with `provider: "codex"`, the event, turn, rollout path, and the PID and creation time of the first `codex*` process among its three nearest ancestors, found on SessionStart and UserPromptSubmit and kept for the turn's later events; it skips `FRICTION_DEBRIEF=1`, and SessionEnd deletes the marker. UserPromptSubmit, PreToolUse, PostToolUse and SessionStart after a compaction mean `running`, PermissionRequest `waiting`, Stop `idle`.

## Rate limits

PRD R111. A turn that stops at a usage or rate limit waits for its reset; evidence in [mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M100.

- **Reading a limit.** Claude: when the marker pass sends a `failed` `rate_limit` or an `idle` marker for a Claude session, the runtime reads the end of its transcript (`readTranscriptLimit` in `packages/claude/src/limit.ts`, the last 512 KiB) and attaches the current turn's limit, if any, to the `marker` event: a refused request's line (`isApiErrorMessage`, `error: "rate_limit"`) gives `kind` `usage` (`apiError: "usage_limit_reached"` or a "hit your … limit" text) or `throttle`, the reset from `apiErrorParams.rate_limit_info.resetsAt` or `quotaLimits.resetsAt` (`exact`), else from its text (`resetFromText`: `resets 1:20pm (America/New_York)` in that zone, `resets Nov 24, 4am (…)`, or a gateway's `reset after 14m43s`); a grace-allowance note (`usageLimitNote`) before a turn that ended normally gives a usage limit without a reset; Claude Code's own wait line after it (`Usage limit reached · continuing automatically at 1:20pm`) sets `cli` and, when nothing else gave one, the reset from its local time. A continue Claude Code already sent starts a new turn, so nothing is read past it. A time in a line's text is read relative to the line's own `timestamp`, so a later read gives the same reset. A roster read that ends a Claude session's turn first carries the limit the same way (`ObservedSession.limit`), so a grace-allowance setup is never finished before its limit is known. Because Claude Code writes its wait line just after the `Stop` hook, each marker pass reads the transcript again, when its size changed, for a Claude session whose turn ended in the last 2 minutes (`LIMIT_READ_MS`), and once after a hub start for any older turn end, and sends `limit-found` when the limit is new or its reset, kind or `cli` changed. Codex app-server: a failed turn whose `codexErrorInfo` is `usageLimitExceeded` (`usage`) or `rateLimitExceeded` (`throttle`) reads as `failed` `rate_limit` with that limit, its reset the latest `resetsAt` of a window at 100% from `account/rateLimits/updated` (`turnLimit` in `appServerRunner.ts`); an update that comes after the failed turn gives a usage limit read without a reset its reset through `limit-found`, and a roster read replays the limit of a thread whose last turn failed so. A `failed` `rate_limit` turn with nothing read is a usage limit with no reset.
- **Keeping it.** The flow stores `Session.rateLimit` (`limitFor` in `packages/core/src/limit.ts`): the reading, when it was found (`at`) and when the first of the run was (`since`), the launch it was found in, the hub's `attempts` and `attemptedAt`, and `nextAt`, the reset plus 3 minutes when Claude Code waits itself (`CLI_MARGIN_MS`; it continued 47 to 93 s after the reset), else plus 1 minute, plus another minute for a time read from text, or 5 minutes on with no reset. A reading of the same launch keeps the attempts and `since`. A new turn keeps the limit, so a limit hit again counts the attempts; a turn that ends without one clears it. A session waits on its limit (`waitsOnLimit`) while it has one and its state is `idle` or `failed`.
- **Its work item.** A held Code Mode session or app-server thread waiting on its limit leaves a `running` task and a `setup` setup as they are, never finishes setup at that turn end, is not ended or released by the idle limit, whether a Code Mode process or an app-server subscription, until it continues or the hub gives up, and is not released. When its work item becomes done or a newer session of it starts, nothing will continue it, so its limit is cleared and it is released or idles out as any session; `limit-found` after a turn end that settled a held task as `finished` or `blocked` runs it again. A background session is released as before: its task is `blocked` with "The turn stopped at a usage limit. Open the session to continue it after the reset.", or its approval waits again with that cause, and a background setup whose process ends at a limit fails with "Setup stopped at a usage limit. Retry it after the reset."; a resumed setup returns to `setup` from that failure or from `blocked`.
- **Continuing.** `Runtime.wake`, the minute timer, sends `tick` and runs `continueLimited`: for each held Code Mode session or app-server thread waiting on its limit whose `nextAt` has come, that is its work item's latest, under the launch the limit was found in, with its work item `setup` or `running` and its ticket not snoozed, one at a time per session. A Code Mode session whose holder reports a key from an attached terminal after the limit was found (the snapshot's `keyAt`, which the hub's own typing never sets) is left to the operator: `limit-skipped` clears the limit and blocks the work item with "You typed in the session during its usage-limit wait, so Switchboard did not continue it." Otherwise `ConversationService.continueAfterLimit` types `CONTINUE_TEXT`, Claude Code's own "Your usage limit has reset. Continue the task you were working on when the limit was reached; do not repeat work that is already complete.", as a `typed` message no window sent, through the same queue and guards as a composer message ([Typing a message](#typing-a-message)), resuming an ended app-server thread first, with a guard (`limitStale`) checked again before each write, so a typing the queue held is not sent once the session continued, its limit changed or its work item stopped running; `limit-attempt` then counts the attempt and moves `nextAt` on by the backoff, 5, 10, 20, 40, then 60 minutes, in case no turn starts. Each `tick` blocks the work item of a session whose sixth attempt's wait passed with no new turn, or whose first attempt was due more than 26 hours ago, with why, and clears the limit. `hub.log` records each attempt and skip.
- **The queue.** `queueWaits` with the work item's provider (`itemProvider`) adds `limit` while a session of that provider waits on a usage limit with a reset still ahead (`providerLimitUntil`), so the scheduler starts nothing in that provider until the reset; a throttle and a limit with no reset hold nothing.
- **Not done.** Codex's `account/rateLimits/read` and `ordinaryUsageAllowed` are not checked before an attempt; a refused attempt waits by the backoff. A Codex exec run's limit and a background Claude session are not continued.

## Terminal holder

PRD R94. `packages/holder` holds Code Mode sessions in pseudo-terminals. It knows nothing about Claude, Code Mode, hooks or work items: `CodeModeRunner` ([Code Mode sessions](#code-mode-sessions)) holds everything Claude-specific. It is built for Windows and Linux; its experiments ran on Windows only ([mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M63–M82). This section is the contract other clients, such as claude-collab's voice front end, code against.

### Responsibilities

- Spawn a program with arguments, working directory, environment and size in a pseudo-terminal (node-pty), under an ID and an opaque label the caller gives.
- Keep each session's screen and scrollback in `@xterm/headless` (10,000 lines), and the first 64 Ki characters of its output, so start evidence outlives scrolling.
- Let clients attach: replay the screen, then stream output; pass their input and size to the program.
- End a session on request by closing its pseudo-terminal, so the program runs its own cleanup, and end its process tree if it has not exited 5 seconds later ([Ending a session](#ending-a-session)); report its exit, and exits it did not cause.
- Keep an exited session's screen for 10 minutes, so a client attaching after the exit sees the last screen and the exit code.
- List every session with its label, pid, folder, start time, last input and output times, attached clients, its process tree and the tree's memory.

Not its job: reading a screen for meaning, deciding when to kill or resume, retrying, resuming after its own restart, or keeping anything but its record.

### Protocol

- **Transport.** TCP on `127.0.0.1`, an ephemeral port, JSON lines in UTF-8.
- **Handshake,** the same in every protocol version. The first line, within 5 seconds, is `{"type": "hello", "token": "<token>"}`; a wrong token or a late or malformed hello closes the connection with `{"type": "error", "message": …}`. A right one is answered `{"type": "welcome", "protocol": <n>, "build": "<build>", "pid": <pid>, "features": [...]}`, where `features` names the requests added within the protocol that this holder serves (`snapshot`, `write`, `view`); a holder from before them sends none, and a client never sends a request the holder does not name, because an unknown type closes the connection. A client checks `pid` against the record and `protocol` against its own; with another protocol it sends only `list`, `kill` and `shutdown`, whose requests and replies are also the same in every version, so `hub.js holder stop` and the dock can stop any session of a holder of another protocol.
- **Token.** One token grants everything: attaching and typing, spawning any program and killing any session (PRD R94).
- **Limits.** A client line over 1 MiB, an unknown type or an invalid field closes the connection with an error; a `session` holds at most 200 characters, a `label` 1,000, a `file` or `cwd` 4,096, and a size at most 1,000 columns or rows. A client accepts reply lines up to 64 MiB, because a `list` or `screen` reply can pass 1 MiB. A replay is sent as `replay` lines of at most 512 KiB each, ended by `replayed`, because a 10,000-line replay is about 1 MiB (mechanics M72), and it is written at the pace the client reads. Other output queued for a client beyond 8 MiB, on its socket or behind its replay, drops that client, never the session. A refused connection is closed one second after its error, even when the peer keeps its side open.
- **Requests,** each with an `id` that the reply echoes as `re`:
  - `spawn {session, label, file, args, cwd, env, cols, rows}` → `spawned {session, pid}`, or `error`, also for a `session` it holds live.
  - `list` → `sessions [{session, label, pid, cwd, startedAt, lastInputAt, lastOutputAt, exitedAt, exitCode, clients, tree, memoryBytes}]`, where `tree` lists the process tree's `{pid, created}`, the creation time telling a reused PID apart, and is absent before the first read. A background sampler reads the process table about every 30 seconds, and `list` answers from the last read (mechanics M75).
  - `kill {session}` → `killed {session, exitCode}` once the process has exited, or `error` after 15 seconds ([Ending a session](#ending-a-session)).
  - `screen {session}` → `screen {session, head, text}`: the retained start of the output and the visible screen as plain text.
  - `snapshot {session, scrollback}` → `snapshot {session, data, cols, rows, rev}`: the screen and up to `scrollback` lines above it (at most 10,000) serialized with their attributes by `@xterm/addon-serialize`, as an attach replays them, once the output already received is parsed; `rev` rises on every output, resize and input, so a reader skips a screen it has read, and `keyAt` is when an attached client last typed. The conversation layer reads replies, colours and the input box from it ([Conversation](#conversation)).
  - `write {session, data, key?}` → `written {session, rev}`: input from a client that is not attached, so no resize and no `clients` count; it sets `lastInputAt` and raises `rev`, and sets `keyAt` only with `key: true`, a key typed in a terminal, and is refused with `error` for a session that is not held or has ended. The hub types the view's messages with it ([Typing a message](#typing-a-message)), and the web page's console its keys with `key` ([Web page](#web-page)).
  - `size {session, cols, rows}` → `sized {session, rev}` (`view` feature): resizes the pseudo-terminal without attaching, refused as `write` is. The hub sends it before the web page console's keys, so the program draws for the console that types.
  - `watch` → `watching`, then a stream of `exited {session, exitCode}`, `attached {session, clients}` and `detached {session, clients}` on this connection.
  - `shutdown` → `error` "The terminal holder holds <n> live sessions." while any process is live; otherwise `bye`, and the holder exits.
- **Attach.** `attach {session, cols, rows}`: the holder resizes the pseudo-terminal to the client's size, so the program redraws for it (mechanics M72), sends the screen and scrollback serialized by `@xterm/addon-serialize`, after a reset of mouse tracking, its encodings and focus reports, because the client reuses its terminal and the addon only turns modes on, and followed by the mouse encoding the program set (DECSET 1006 or 1016), which the addon leaves out (mechanics M101), as `replay {data}` lines and `replayed`, then `output {data}` as the program writes. The client sends `input {data}` and `resize {cols, rows}`. On exit the holder sends `exited {exitCode}` and closes. The last client to attach, resize or type sets the size; the headless screen resizes after the output already queued, which was drawn at the old size. Input from several clients interleaves; the holder does not lock input.
- **View** (`view` feature). `view {session}`: the holder sends `resized {cols, rows}`, the size the program draws at, then the replay as an attach does and `replayed`, then `output {data}` with a `resized` at each resize, in its place among the output, and `exited` at the end. A viewer never resizes the pseudo-terminal and is not counted in `clients` or `attached`; `input`, `resize` or any request from it closes the connection with an error. The hub watches a session for the web page's console with it ([Web page](#web-page)).
- **Versions.** A field added within a protocol is optional, so an older client keeps working; any other change is a new `protocol` number.

### Record and lock

- `<home>/holder.json` holds `protocol`, `build`, `port`, `token` (32 random bytes, hex), `pid` and `startedAt`, written only by the lock holder and readable by the operator only, as `hub.json` is.
- The lock is the named pipe `\\.\pipe\switchboard-holder-<hash of home>` on Windows and `<home>/holder.sock` elsewhere. A holder that cannot take it exits, after trying for 2 seconds while the recorded holder does not answer, because a holder ended by force can keep its pipe for a moment. A client accepts a record only when `welcome` answers with its pid, as for a hub record a forced exit left.

### Lifecycle

- **Start.** `HolderLink.find(true)` (`packages/hub/src/holder.ts`), called before a Code Mode start or resume, reads `holder.json` and checks it with `hello`, keeping one connection while it lasts. When none answers and no process holds the lock, it reserves the build `current.json` names and runs `<sessions.nodePath> "<home>/holder/<build>/holder.js" --home=<home> --build=<build>` detached and hidden, without VS Code's `ELECTRON_*` and `VSCODE_*` variables and `NODE_OPTIONS`, with output appended to `<home>/holder.log`, and waits up to 10 seconds for the record; a failure fails the session's start with the reason and the log's path. The holder runs on the operator's Node.js, not VS Code's runtime, because node-pty is a native module. The hub's bundle imports only the holder client, which has no native dependency, so a missing or broken native module fails only Code Mode starts (mechanics M76).
- **Install.** Activation installs each build in its own folder, `<home>/holder/<build>/`: `holder.js` with the pinned node-pty's `lib` helpers, including its worker and console-list agent, and its prebuilt binaries, because Windows refuses to replace a loaded `.node` file while a second build in another folder runs beside it (mechanics M76). The build hashes every file's path and content into `manifest.json`, and activation names the installed build in `<home>/holder/current.json`, which the hub starts. Activation installs under an install lock, the named pipe `\\.\pipe\switchboard-holder-install-<hash of home>` on Windows and `<home>/holder/install.sock` elsewhere, so windows activating together take turns; it never replaces a complete installed build, and copies again one that misses a listed file, moving the old folder aside. It removes build folders other than the current one, the running holder's and one whose `in-use` file the hub touched, under the same lock, in the last 10 minutes before starting a holder from it. A build folder is one named by a build, 16 lowercase hex digits, or a copy an install left, `<build>.<pid>.tmp` or `<build>.<pid>.<time>.old`; every other entry of the folder stays. A failed install is written to `hub.log` and fails only Code Mode starts.
- **Hub restart, extension update, VS Code closing.** The holder and its sessions keep running. A hub accepts any holder that speaks its `protocol`. A holder of another protocol that holds live sessions is kept, listed through `list` and stopped through `kill`, and Code Mode starts and resumes fail with "The terminal holder is from another version of Switchboard; it is replaced once its <n> sessions end."; once it holds none, the hub sends `shutdown` and starts the new one.
- **Idle exit.** The holder exits 10 minutes after it last held a live process, whatever clients, `watch` or attach, are connected. The hub starts it again at the next Code Mode start or resume.
- **Exit.** Before it exits, at the idle time, on `shutdown` or on `SIGINT` or `SIGTERM`, the holder refuses further spawns with "The terminal holder is exiting." and ends each live session as `kill` does; idle and `shutdown` exits come only when none is live.
- **Crash or logoff.** The pseudo-terminals close with the holder, and each `claude` exits within seconds (mechanics M69), but a shell command it was running can run on, unlike after a `kill` (M78); the hub applies [holder loss](#code-mode-sessions).

### Ending a session

Every end the hub asks for is a `kill`: a release after the turn, when the work item is done or a newer session starts, the idle end, Stop, Delete and `hub.js holder stop`. `Host` ends the program in two steps under the one 15-second deadline (`KILL_DEADLINE_MS`):

- **Close.** `closePseudoTerminal` (`packages/holder/src/close.ts`) closes the session's pseudo-terminal and leaves the program running. Claude Code then ends its shell commands, logs `SessionEnd` `other` and exits with 129 within about 2 seconds (mechanics M78). A process a command left behind outside the tree, such as M78's `sleep.exe`, runs on (PRD Q10). On Windows the holder keeps reading the output, so the last screen, with what Claude wrote while it cleaned up, stays attachable; on Linux the close ends the reading, so the screen is the one before the close. The holder passes no input or size to a closed session. A second `kill` during the close waits for the same exit and does not close again.
- **Tree kill.** When the program has not exited 5 seconds after the close (`CLOSE_GRACE_MS`), or the pseudo-terminal cannot be closed, the holder ends the process tree: `taskkill /T /F` on Windows, `SIGKILL` to each member elsewhere, as [Codex runner](#codex-runner) does. That skips Claude's cleanup and `SessionEnd`. It logs "Session <id> did not exit within 5 seconds of its terminal closing; ending its process tree." or "The terminal of session <id> cannot be closed; ending its process tree.".
- **Windows.** node-pty's own `kill()` kills `claude` before its cleanup can run (M78), so the close calls `ClosePseudoConsole` through node-pty's private agent, `_agent._ptyNative.kill(_agent._pty, false)`, after checking that those fields exist, and only for ConPTY without `conpty.dll`, whose native kill also terminates the program. node-pty is pinned in `packages/holder/package.json`; the built-holder test, whose program records a hang-up, fails when an upgrade breaks the close, and the holder falls back to the tree kill meanwhile.
- **Linux.** node-pty's `destroy()`, which closes the master side and sends the program `SIGHUP`. Not verified on Linux.

### Visibility of held sessions

- **CLI.** `hub.js holder list`, `hub.js holder stop <id>` and `hub.js holder stop --all [--yes]` (`runHolder` in `packages/hub/src/holderCli.ts`) take their home from `--home`, else from the bundle's location when it is `<home>/.switchboard/hub.js`, as `report` does, else the user's home folder, so the extension's own `dist/hub.js` reaches the same hub and holder (`bundleHome`). With the hub up they post `POST /holder/list`, which also answers the connected holder's `welcome` as `holder`, and `POST /holder/stop {id}`. A holder that lacks a request this build sends (`olderHolder`), as one from an older build lacks `write`, is named first, from the hub's answer or its own `welcome`: "Terminal holder: pid …, build …, protocol …; it is from an older build and cannot type into sessions, and `holder stop --all` ends it so the next session starts this build.", or "…and cannot show its sessions in the web page's console…" for one that lacks only `view`. `list` prints a table of every held session, orphans first: the work item key, or "No work item" for an orphan no session of the store names, the Switchboard and Claude session IDs, folder, state, idle time (`3m`, `2h 5m`, or `-` once exited), memory in MB and attached terminals; "No held sessions." when there are none. With the hub down it reads `holder.json`, connects with `HolderClient` whatever the holder's protocol, after checking that the welcome's `pid` is the record's (`connectHolder`), and prints the holder's own list after a line saying so: the state is `live` or `exited`, and the work item key and Claude session ID come from the label (`labelParts`), so after an ID change the new Claude ID is known only to the hub; with no record or no answer it prints "No terminal holder is running.". `stop` takes a Switchboard session ID, a Claude session ID, current or retired, or exactly its first 8 characters (`findHeld`), refusing an ID that names no held session, a prefix that names several, with their Switchboard session IDs, and an exited session. With the hub up it stops through the hub, which resolves current IDs: `Runtime.stopHeld` sends `stop` with the session's work item and `sessionId` for an active session, so the flow records "Stopped by you." and an attach waits for the stop as for any stop of a held session ([Code Mode sessions](#code-mode-sessions)), waits for a resume in progress, whose session is still `ended` while its spawn runs, and then stops it through the flow, and kills an orphan in the holder directly, logging "stopped orphaned held session <id>: <label>". With the hub down it kills through the holder, matching the Switchboard session ID or the Claude ID the label gives; the hub's next roster read ends the session. `stop --all` names the holder and the live sessions that end with it, each of which can be resumed, and without `--yes` ends nothing. With `--yes` it stops each live session, through the hub when it answers, so an active one stops through the flow, else with the holder's `kill`, which closes the session's pseudo-terminal and ends its process tree; then it sends the holder `shutdown` over its own connection and, when the holder's process has not exited within 10 seconds, as when a session did not stop and the holder refused, ends its process tree (`taskkill /T /F`). The hub's connection closes with the holder, its next lookup finds the lock free, and the next Code Mode start launches the installed build ([Terminal holder](#terminal-holder)). Exit codes: 0 done, 1 usage or a request the hub refused as malformed, 2 for `stop` when neither the hub nor a holder answers, 3 a refusal, an unknown ID or a failed holder call.
- **Orphans.** A live held process whose `session` is no active session of the store, such as after a failed Delete stop or a store reset, or one whose session ended while it still runs. A known older session still held, such as one whose end waits for its terminal to detach, is not an orphan, nor is a session whose resume spawn runs. Each roster read, whether or not a Code Mode session is active, ends with one holder `list` through `HolderLink.find(false)`, which never starts a holder, unless `holder.json` is missing; a holder that is gone gives an empty list, and a failed read keeps the last list and logs "held session list failed: <error>" once per error. The hub logs each orphan once, "held session <id> has no work item: <label>", lists it and offers Stop; it never kills one by itself.
- **Snapshot.** `held` (`HeldSession` in `packages/hub/src/held.ts`), absent when the holder lists none, is the holder's last list joined with the store, orphans first: `session`, `label`, `workItemId` (null when no session of the store has the ID), `key` (the work item's, else the label's), `nativeSessionId` (the store's current, else the label's), `cwd`, `state` (the store's for an active session, else `live` or `exited`), `idleSince` (the latest of the start, input and output), `memoryBytes`, `clients`, `exitedAt`, `exitCode` and `orphan`. The dock view turns it into `held`, `DockHeld` rows with the columns `hub.js holder list` prints (`heldTexts`) and, for a session of the store that is not an orphan, its attach terminal, and adds `heldNote` while any is an orphan ([Dock](#dock)).

### Attach client

`hub.js attach <Switchboard session ID>` (`runAttach` in `packages/hub/src/attach.ts`), run with `sessions.nodePath`, takes its home from the bundle's location, as `report` does, or from `--home`. It reads `hub.json` and posts `POST /attach {sessionId}` with a 90-second timeout, because a resume may start the holder and wait for its spawn; the hub takes an attach reservation, resumes an ended session and answers `{port, token, session}`, or the refusal. It connects to the holder with `Attachment.open` at the terminal's size, puts the terminal in raw mode, writes the replay and output, and passes input and size changes. When the session exits it leaves raw mode and prints "The session ended (exit code <n>). Press Enter to resume it, or close this terminal."; Enter attaches again, which resumes the session, so the terminal a window or the terminal app keeps for the session is the one Open in terminal shows again. A refusal prints "Refused: <error>", a hub that is down or does not answer prints why, and a connection the holder closes or refuses prints its reason, each followed by "Press Enter to try again, or close this terminal." It exits 0 when its input ends and 1 on a usage error; closing it only detaches.

## Conversation

A Code Mode session's conversation in its item window's Conversation view (PRD R96, R97): the operator reads the replies beside the session's terminal, types messages in the terminal or in the view's composer, reads the session's latest status on the view's Status tab (PRD R98), and opens the artifacts it writes, listed on its Artifacts tab (PRD R99), each marked unread until seen (PRD R100), switches between the window's work item and the conversations started from it (PRD R102), and answers the lettered questions a session asks with a card (PRD R103). The replies to its own messages are read aloud while the voice speaks for its session (PRD R105, [Voice](#voice)), and the operator can dictate messages to it (PRD R106, [Dictation](#dictation)).

### Conversation rules

A Code Mode session's `--append-system-prompt` ends with `conversationRules(artifactsDir)` (`packages/claude/src/conversationRules.ts`): start text written only to show progress with `[aside]`, start a reply that replaces the previous one after a newer message with `[rewrite]`, write a status, which the view shows on its own tab beside the conversation and which replaces the one before it, after a `[status]` line of its own, to the end of that text, with any reply before the tag, that text written directly before a tool call in the same response can be saved only as thinking, so it never reaches the view, that the view draws numbered questions whose options are lettered from A, each on its own line as a nested list item, put at the end of the message, as a card whose answer comes as an ordinary message such as "1A, 2B" ([Conversation view](#conversation-view)), with an example, and not to ask with AskUserQuestion, even where the report contract or a skill says to, because its menu holds the whole session and the operator's messages until it is answered in the terminal and the view cannot show it, but to ask in a reply, whose unanswered card keeps the work item waiting on the operator when the turn ends (PRD R103); they no longer ask for a reply at the end of the turn, so a turn may write several replies between its tool calls (voice-design decision 24). An "Artifacts" section ([Artifacts](#artifacts)) names the session's artifact folder by its path and its variable, `CLAUDE_ARTIFACTS_DIR`, and explains the `<owner>/<kind>[-<slug>].<ext>` names, how each type opens, the folder beside an artifact for the files it uses, front matter, rewriting the same file to update it and `` ```mermaid `` blocks, and says it does not say when to write one. The rules only explain the tags the view reads and say so; they set no length, shape, format or verbosity, so a session writes as it would without them, with Claude Code's default verbosity and the operator's output style (voice-design decision 21). Each slice adds the rules for what it reads. No rules version is kept: the reader reads every tag of every session as if it carries the current rules, and the contract hook gives a resumed session, whose start prompt Claude keeps, the current rules, and a compacted one the contract and the current rules ([Claude Code runner](#claude-code-runner)). The contract hook's text names the same artifact folder, from the session's folder and work item. With `voice.enabled` on, a "Spoken replies" section (`SPOKEN_RULES`) comes before the artifact rules, read at each start, resume and compaction: a message that starts with `[spoken]` will have its reply heard; write that reply as otherwise and mark the part to read with `[speak]`…`[/speak]`, a few plain sentences, usually its opening, with no lists, tables, code, paths, hashes or URLs; each text of a turn may mark its own passage, read in order; a reply with no passage is not read; a message that also carries `[expressive]` gets a first-line tone line, `[tone: …]`, never read or shown (voice-design decisions 21, 24). A "Dictated messages" section (`DICTATION_RULES`) follows it, also only with `voice.enabled`: a message that starts with `[voice]` was dictated, so its words come from speech-to-text and are read loosely; a `[voice] [partial]` message is the next part of a long message still being spoken, not to be answered or acknowledged, and the `[voice]` message that ends it carries the rest, answered with the parts as one message; a `[voice]` message that clearly isn't addressed to the session, such as talk to someone else in the room or background audio, is not acted on and gets only a short `[aside]` saying it was ignored; and a misheard name or technical term gets a heard tag, `<!--heard:what came through=what was meant-->`, once per mishearing, which the view hides ([Dictation](#dictation)). A session with a [persona](#personas) gets its tone section last, after the artifact rules.

### Window scope

A window sees only its own conversations, enforced in the hub (voice-design decision 26):

- **Key.** Each window stream's `hello` carries a window key (`WindowScopes` in `packages/hub/src/windows.ts`), 24 random bytes, which dies with the stream; a reload gets a new one. The hub derives everything from the key, so no conversation route takes a work item ID except `select`, which names one of the window's own conversations, or for the web page any work item, which then binds it ([Web page](#web-page)).
- **Binding.** The key's workspace file binds the window. Its **primary work item** is the newest work item that `windowItems` gives for the file and that is not done, else the newest, because successive work items of a ticket share its worktree and window; `windowItems` leaves out the hosted conversations, so a new conversation never becomes the primary. The window's **conversations** are the primary, while it exists, then the ad hoc work items whose `Ticket.hostWindow` is the window's file ([Hosted conversations](#hosted-conversations)) that are not done, newest first, and the selected one even when it is done, until another is selected. Membership never follows a folder: another work item in the same folder, as every Support work item is, never joins. The binding is kept in the `windowBindings` table by `windowFileKey` of the file: the primary, the ticket and title it showed, so a deleted primary still names them, the **selected conversation** and its current session, and `scopeGen`, so a reload keeps them.
